A custom-render frame that bypasses the literary layout entirely, dispatching from `render` into its own `render_chrono_frame`.

    **Step up from questline.** Where questline is flat NES bands + chunky monospace Press Start 2P, chrono shows the three things 8-bit hardware couldn't — **gradient-shaded backgrounds**, a **translucent-blue gradient dialogue window with a rounded beveled border**, and a **character portrait sub-window** — in the finer, multi-weight Pixelify Sans.

    **Sky.** `_chrono_paint_sky` paints an atmospheric twilight: a per-row Bayer **density-ramp gradient** (deep navy B+K at the zenith fading to pure blue at mid-sky, then a rising white-dither haze toward the horizon — the continuous tone flat 8-bit bands can't produce), ~70 deterministic white stars (`_CHRONO_STAR_SEED`, cleared from the moon's disc), a pale white moon with faint sky-blue (B+W) craters, and a **two-range mountain silhouette** (a solid-blue back range + a navy B+K-dithered front range via `_chrono_fill_poly`) for parallax depth.

    **Window.** The quote sits in the iconic **FF blue window**: `_chrono_window_fill` paints a navy→blue→faint-sky-blue vertical gradient onto a private tile and pastes it through a rounded-rectangle mask so the corners read soft; `_chrono_window_border` strokes a white rounded double frame with small yellow corner-accent ticks.

    **Hourglass portrait.** A **portrait sub-window** (`_chrono_paint_portrait`, the same window recipe at smaller scale) carries an ornate, *shaded* **hourglass** — the "portrait" a cutscene frames beside the dialogue, and thematically exact for `chrono` (= time). A geometric object renders cleanly and lets the tonal-dither trick do real work. It uses **multi-tone shading ramps synthesised from the six inks** (`_CHRONO_ART_TONES`: cream→gold→bronze brass via Y+W / Y / R+Y, amber sand at varying R+Y density, sky-tint glass via B+W), so the brass, glass and sand read as rounded, lit volumes rather than flat shapes.

    **Hourglass build.** `_chrono_build_hourglass` sculpts it with **vector shapes at a low logical resolution** — a brass frame (capped top/bottom with finials + two shaded side posts), two sky-tinted glass bulbs with specular streaks, and amber sand draining from a dished surface in the upper bulb through the neck as a thin stream onto a mound in the lower bulb; light from the upper-left — drawn into a tone-indexed `L` image. `_chrono_paint_hourglass` upscales it (NEAREST) and dither-maps each tone index to its Spectra-6 colour at absolute panel coordinates, so the synthesised tones share a continuous Bayer phase with the rest of the frame. This shape-plus-dither approach is what gets the rounded SNES look out of a 6-ink panel — a flat hand-pixelled sprite cannot.

    **Dialogue.** `_chrono_paint_dialogue` prints the **author as a white Pixelify Sans Bold speaker-name header**, then the quote left-aligned below with the matched time-phrase in **yellow Pixelify Sans Bold** — a real weight step on top of the accent colour, the payoff of the variable font (questline's single-weight Press Start 2P differentiates on colour alone). A yellow ▼ continue arrow sits bottom-right, and a white `— from {Title} —` footer runs along the window foot.

    **No digital time.** Like `questline` / `marquee`, the digital HH:MM is **never surfaced** — the matched phrase carries the time; `time_str` is `del`-asserted at the top of `render_chrono_frame`. Saturation tier `0.7` (gradient-blue ground). Stays in the button-B / auto / random rotation. No `_DEBUG_LABEL_RIGHT_INSET` entry — custom frame, ignores `mode`.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - **A face in the portrait** — a hand-built frontal face reads as crude on a 6-ink panel, and a shaded anime face in an earlier revision lost to the hourglass, which reads cleaner on the panel and ties the portrait to the theme.
    - **A sky-blue B+W dither for the footer:** it averaged too close to the window fill to stay legible at the footer point size.

    **Its own sleep frame** (`render_chrono_sleep`, the theme's `spec.sleep`): **the End of Time**, Chrono Trigger's hub between eras. A black void with a faint blue haze rising to the horizon (a `BAYER_8x8` ramp; the 4×4 tile banded visibly) and a seeded scatter of motes, a stone platform stippled navy with a white rim, and one lamppost whose lantern blooms yellow into the void through `paint_neon_mask`, and a pool of lamplight stippled across the platform from a feathered ellipse read against `_bayer_threshold_field`, clipped to the platform so it stops at the rim. The pool was first a core-less `paint_neon_mask`, which paints only the halo *round* its mask: it came out as a hollow ring spilling into the void (caught in review). `ground=` keeps the lantern's bloom off the post, which is drawn black over it. The portrait's hourglass has **run out** (`_chrono_build_hourglass(run_out=True)` empties the upper bulb into a full mound below, leaving the default sculpt untouched), and the narrator says "Rest here, traveller; the gates open again at dawn" with **dawn** in the matched-phrase yellow. That promise of dawn and the lit lamp are what keep "the End of Time" reading as resting rather than ended. A dozing Gaspar against the post was mocked up and dropped: the lamp alone carries the scene and a figure would have needed shading to match the portrait. A campfire alternative was rejected as a second RPG party resting, too close to `questline`'s inn. Composed at 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled; nothing on it reads the time.
  - `outrun` (black ground / white Oxanium body / synthesised-magenta matched-phrase) — a **1980s synthwave / Outrun sunset**. A custom-render frame that bypasses the literary layout entirely, dispatching from `render` into its own `render_outrun_frame`. The "neon" counterpart to the cool digital themes (`nightvision` terminal-green, `chrono` JRPG-blue): where those are screens, outrun is a *poster*. Layers, deepest → shallowest, follow.

    **(1) Sky.** `_outrun_paint_sky` is a two-zone per-row Bayer **density-ramp gradient**: a continuous deep-indigo-navy zenith (blue + a heavy black wash) → pure blue mid-sky → red-leaning **magenta horizon** (blue + red, red capped at 0.36 so it stays a blue-leaning violet rather than tipping into fire-engine red — red out-luminates blue, so an even mix reads hot). The zone seam is placed exactly where the navy ramp reaches pure blue (black density 0), so it's continuous. All the *warm* colour is reserved for the sun, keeping the quote's white-on-cool contrast high. ~70 deterministic white stars (`_OUTRUN_STAR_SEED`) are confined to the dark upper sky above the sun cap.

    **(2) Grid.** `_outrun_paint_grid`: the dark near-black ground (faint-blue Bayer specks) carries the **neon perspective grid** — magenta verticals fanning from a central vanishing point on the horizon and cyan horizontals marching toward the foot with perspective spacing (y = horizon + span·t², so lines pack near the horizon), plus a bright white horizon line with a cyan under-glow. PIL has no per-pixel line colour, so each grid line is plotted via an integer **Bresenham** point list (`_outrun_line_points`) and coloured per-pixel — verticals alternate red/blue (→ magenta), horizontals alternate green/blue (→ cyan) — so the two-tone neon reads solid at panel distance while staying entirely on the Spectra-6 palette.

    **(3) Sun.** `_outrun_paint_sun` draws the **sliced retrowave half-sun**. The disc centre (`_OUTRUN_SUN_CENTER`) sits *below* the horizon, so only the upper cap shows, rising from behind the grid. The visible cap carries a yellow→tangerine→magenta-foot vertical gradient mapped across its *visible* height (golden crown via R+Y at density 0.60, tangerine body via the documented R+Y 5/8:3/8 recipe, magenta foot via R+B melting back into the sky), sliced by horizontal **slits** (left unpainted so the sky shows through) that *widen toward the horizon* — the canonical sliced-sun silhouette.

    **(4) Quote.** `_outrun_paint_quote` floats the literary quote in the dark upper sky (`_OUTRUN_QUOTE_RECT`) in solid white Oxanium, centred line-by-line via the shared `fit_quote` / `wrap_styled_text` pipeline. The matched time-phrase is a 5/8:3/8 red+blue **magenta** stipple (`draw_text_dithered`) — the synthwave neon-pink accent, biased toward red (not 50/50 violet) so it stays crisp against the navy/blue sky instead of melting into the blue ground, and tied to the magenta grid verticals. The body stays solid white for maximum legibility on the navy ground.

    **(5) Credits.** `_outrun_paint_credits` sets the author (white) + `— title —` (magenta) below the quote in the condensed Antonio poster-credit face the other custom frames reuse, in the clear sky band between the quote and the rising sun.

    **Frame rules.** Like `questline` / `chrono` / `marquee`, the digital HH:MM is **never surfaced** — the matched phrase carries the time; `time_str` is `del`-asserted at the top of `render_outrun_frame`. Every raw PixelAccess write is bounds-clipped so the `/api/preview` thumbnail path (down to ~60 px tall) crops the lower composition rather than raising — the defensive pattern of the chrono / diags frames. Fully deterministic (the only RNG is the seeded star field), so re-renders of a given time are byte-identical.

    **Face.** **Oxanium** (Sev Meyer, OFL) — a techno / cyberpunk display sans whose squared geometric terminals read as period-correct 1980s retro-future; it has a variable Weight axis, so the matched-phrase `quote_bold` role pins the Bold instance for a real weight step on top of the magenta accent. Saturation tier `0.7` (black ground — the neon tones need the dark-ground push). Stays in the button-B / auto / random rotation. No `_DEBUG_LABEL_RIGHT_INSET` entry — custom frame, ignores `mode`.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - **A green-biased green+blue teal matched phrase:** teal read dim and illegible against the cool sky.
    - **Orbitron** (the canonical synthwave face): too wide to hold a dense 200-char literary quote at a legible size across a room; Oxanium's narrower proportions keep long lines readable while preserving the neon-future silhouette.
  - `anna_atkins` (deep Prussian-blue cyanotype ground / ghostly white algae-photogram silhouettes / white Libre Caslon Text body / sky-blue matched phrase) — **Anna Atkins 1843 botanical cyanotype**, *Photographs of British Algae: Cyanotype Impressions*, the first book ever illustrated with photographs. A literary-layout theme (not a custom-render frame) with a heavy `draw_anna_atkins_border` painter.

    **Render-time image dithering.** **The theme that introduces the repo's render-time image-dithering capability** (`dither_image_to_palette` + `_load_dithered_plate`). Rather than synthesising every graphic from ImageDraw primitives, a committed *continuous-tone* PNG (`idle_hours/assets/anna_atkins_cyanotype.png`, produced by `scripts/generate_anna_atkins_plate.py`) is Floyd–Steinberg-dithered down to the inks at render time, giving photographic tonal fidelity on the 6-colour panel — the deep blues break into a blue/black stipple, the feathered specimen edges melt through a blue+white "sky-blue" haze, and the bright cores stay white.

    **The plate.** The committed plate is an *original work in the cyanotype idiom* rather than a museum scan: Atkins's photographs are public domain, but the historical scans live behind image hosts the build environment can't reach, and the point of the asset is to exercise the dithering path, which needs real continuous-tone content — drop a genuine scan in at the same path and it dithers identically. The plate is dithered against only the **white/black/blue** cyanotype sub-palette, not the full Spectra-6 set, so error diffusion can't scatter stray red/green specks across the deep blues; it is still a strict subset of SPECTRA6, so the final `snap_image_to_palette` is a no-op.

    **Layers, deepest → shallowest.** (1) **Layer 0** — the full-bleed FS-dithered photogram (or, if the asset is missing, the flat blue `page_bg` deepened to Prussian via a blue→black Bayer stipple so the theme still renders). (2) **Four procedural white fern/algae sprigs** in the side margins — crisp 1 px ink-line accents (a curving rachis with pinnate leaflets via `_anna_atkins_fern`) layered over the soft photographic specimens. (3) **Handwritten white Latin species labels** in copperplate (Pinyon Script — "Cystoseira fibrosa", "Ptilota plumosa", "Dictyota dichotoma"), slightly rotated and stamped *binary* (an L-mask rotated then pasted solid-white where opaque, so the rotated antialiased edges can't leave a blue halo after palette snapping), the way Atkins captioned each plate in her own hand. (4) A **thin white plate rule** at inset 12 (the mounting-sheet edge). (5) **No body panel** — see below.

    **No body panel.** Unlike `kanagawa` / `cartograph` / `circuit`, it takes **no `clear_rect` knockout**: the quote is rendered directly over the photogram, and legibility comes from a subtle **per-glyph black halo** — a 2 px black `stroke_width` pass stamped in `_draw_text_body` before the real white / sky-blue glyph goes down on top, so each letter sits inside a thin dark ring. It is also the truer object: a real cyanotype carries its caption written straight on the sheet, not on a card laid over it. This is why `anna_atkins`'s spec deliberately sets no `clear_rect_pad` (a comment on the spec says so) — adding one would restore the panel.

    **Matched phrase.** The `accent` is a **yellow sentinel**, not a painted colour: `_draw_text_body` reroutes the matched-phrase yellow to a 50/50 blue+white **sky-blue** stipple, so the time phrase reads as a ghostly cyanotype element (paler, cooler, stippled), distinct from the crisp solid-white body. The sentinel surfaces literally only in the debug-mode banner (yellow-on-blue, legible), the same accent-as-debug-banner pattern `firmament` uses on its navy ground. The oversized opening/closing quote marks render as the same sky-blue stipple (ornament_dark/light = blue/white via `draw_faux_gray_text`).

    **Distinctness.** Distinct from the rotation's other blue themes by construction: `firmament` is a navy *celestial* atlas — anna_atkins is the only *blue-ground photographic* theme. Saturation tier `0.7` (deep-blue non-white ground).

    **Plate reuse.** The first theme to dither a committed raster image; `letter` (white/yellow/red/green aged-paper plate), `daguerreotype` (white+black silver plate, Atkinson) and `control` (white+black board-formed concrete for its plinth) now reuse the same `dither_image_to_palette` + `_load_dithered_plate` path, each restricted to its own sub-palette and falling back to a synthesised stipple when the asset is absent. `autochrome` is the one that does **not** restrict, dithering a colour photograph against all six inks because there the chroma is the subject.

    **Tried and rejected.** Two knockout body panels — a Prussian-blue one at the theme's introduction, then a frosted translucent-grey one. Both failed the same way, because the failure is geometric rather than chromatic: a panel big enough to hold a dense quote covers ~62% of the canvas (663×359 of 800×480), so whatever it is filled with, most of the specimen field — the theme's whole point — sits behind a rectangle. Tinting it more faintly does not help; only removing it does. The halo buys the same contrast at the cost of a 2 px ring per glyph.
  - `lieder` (cream-washed white / roman-black engraving / italic-black editorial marks / red sung phrase, Alegreya + Noto Music) — **an engraved art-song manuscript**, the quote as the vocal line of a *Lied*. A custom-render frame, bypassing the literary layout entirely, dispatching from `render` into its own `render_lieder_frame`.

    **The score carries the clock.** The words are engraved as **lyrics beneath a five-line stave, one notehead per word**, and — uniquely in the rotation — the clock is carried by the score's own furniture rather than by an ornament that merely *encodes* it. The **time signature is the hour over 4** (7 o'clock → 7/4) and, because a time signature genuinely governs the measure length, the hour also decides **where the barlines fall** — a 3 o'clock page is densely barred, a 12 o'clock page wide open. The **tempo mark is the minute** — an Italian tempo word from `_LIEDER_TEMPO_WORDS` plus a metronome mark of `60 + minute` BPM, which walks Larghetto → Allegro across the hour and stays musically plausible at every minute (a literal `♩ = 3` at three minutes past would be nonsense). This is the reason `time_str` is *used* here, where `questline` / `chrono` / `outrun` / `marquee` `del`-assert it; the digital HH:MM is still never printed as digits, the matched phrase carrying the readable time as everywhere else.

    **Text → notes.** `_lieder_notes` splits the quote into *syllables*, one note each, via `_lieder_syllables`. That splitter is a heuristic and deliberately so — a correct English syllabifier needs a pronunciation dictionary, and the failure that matters musically is the syllable **count** (it decides how many notes a word gets), not the split point: a reader forgives `ga-ther` for `gath-er`, but a three-note setting of a two-syllable word is simply wrong. Rules, in order of importance: `qu` is a consonant (so `quiet` has one vowel run, not two); a final silent `e` is not a syllable unless it is the `-le` of `ta-ble`; `-ed` is silent except after t/d; `-es` is silent except after a sibilant; a digraph (`th`/`sh`/`ch`/`wh`/`ph`/`gh`/`ck`/`ng`/`qu`) never splits; a doubled consonant always splits between the pair. `_LIEDER_SYLLABLE_EXCEPTIONS` covers the rest, and was **derived by scanning the 150 most frequent multi-syllable words in the committed corpus**, not invented — every hyphenation engine ships a list like this (TeX calls it `\hyphenation{}`).

    **Rhythm.** `_lieder_rhythm` gives each syllable a duration from its stress — a phrase-final syllable is held (a dotted half at the very end), a stressed one takes a quarter, a weak one an eighth — which is what gives the line speech rhythm rather than a flat stream of equal notes. Stress comes from `_LIEDER_FUNCTION_WORDS` (unstressed by definition) and first-syllable-of-a-content-word, with `_LIEDER_WEAK_PREFIXES` moving the accent to syllable two for `a-BOUT` / `be-FORE` / `re-TURN`.

    **Bars fill exactly.** Bars are filled to **exactly** `numerator` beats: a note is shortened rather than allowed to overflow, because a note straddling a barline has to be written as a tie and an untied one is wrong notation. The final bar is completed in two steps, because a single notehead can only express a handful of durations — the last syllable grows as far as one notated duration allows, and whatever is still missing is padded with **rests**, which is exactly what an engraver does when a voice stops mid-measure. `tests/test_render_quote_themes.py::TestLiederRhythm` sweeps every corpus row against all twelve meters and fails on either an incomplete final bar or a note that crosses a barline; the meter *is* the hour, so all twelve are reachable in normal operation and none of it is hypothetical.

    **Melody.** `_lieder_contour` walks the pitch in staff positions (a "position" is a half-space; 0 is the bottom line, 8 the top) from a `random.Random` seeded by `_row_digest` — an FNV-1a digest of `(source_id, line_number, display_quote)`, **not** `hash()`, which is PYTHONHASHSEED-salted and would make the same quote render a different melody per process, breaking both the golden fixture and run_clock's "quote unchanged, skip the redraw" dedup. Four rules give the walk direction:
    - **Downbeat gravity** pulls the first note of each bar onto a tonic-triad degree (in this key — no key signature, so C major — every staff position is a diatonic degree and the triad sits on degrees 0/2/4, so the nudge is never more than one step).
    - **Leap resolution** forces a step in the *opposite* direction after any leap of three degrees or more; this is the oldest rule in voice-leading and is most of what makes a line feel sung rather than generated.
    - **Phrase resolution** settles a phrase-final syllable onto a chord tone.
    - **Cadence** lands the final note on the tonic, approached by step — the single biggest difference, since a melody ending anywhere else sounds interrupted however well-formed the rest is.

    The matched phrase keeps its upward bias so it still peaks, and the restoring pull at either edge of the range still applies.

    **Engraving.** Notehead shape carries duration (void for a half or dotted half, filled otherwise) and `_lieder_beam_groups` **beams** runs of consecutive eighths rather than flagging each one: modern vocal engraving beams across separate syllables, and at a 7 px staff gap a page of single flags reads as noise. Runs break at a barline (a beam never crosses one) and cap at four, so a stream of weak syllables groups by beat instead of growing one enormous beam; a whole group shares one stem direction (majority side of the middle line) and one horizontal beam clear of every head in it. Isolated eighths still get a flag, which always hangs to the *right* of the stem regardless of stem direction. Stems otherwise turn at the middle line. Lyric hyphens are centred in the gap between syllables of a word, breath marks sit above the staff at phrase ends, and augmentation dots move up a half-space when the note sits on a line so the dot lands in a space.

    **Spacing.** Notes are spaced by `_lieder_slot`, which takes the larger of the syllable's own text width and a duration-proportional share — real engraving spaces by duration but *compresses* the relation (a half note gets more room than an eighth, nowhere near four times more), which is the 0.6 exponent.

    **Fitting.** `_lieder_fit` shrinks the lyric font against *two* constraints: the notes must wrap into at most `_LIEDER_MAX_SYSTEMS` (3) staves, and those staves must fit the vertical band — a system is much taller than a line of prose (headroom for stems and the slur above, the staff, then the lyric below), so a size that wraps to three lines can still overrun the page. The lyric size is capped in a narrow band (26..13) rather than scaled freely the way `fit_quote` scales the literary layout: in engraving the lyric size tracks the staff size, so letting a short quote balloon to 60pt over a 28px staff would read as a caption pasted onto a score — variance is absorbed by the *number of systems* instead. Wrapping never splits a word across two staves. Lyrics are drawn with `anchor="ms"` so every word shares one baseline (positioning by ink-bbox top instead lets a word with no ascender float above its neighbours).

    **Ink discipline is typographic before chromatic.** Roman black is the plate: staves, clef, notes, barlines, lyrics, title. Italic black is the editorial apparatus: tempo, expression, composer. It stays solid black because a synthesised two-ink tone shatters Alegreya's italic hairlines at 13 px after palette snapping. Red is the sung phrase alone: its lyric, noteheads and slur. Layer 0 is the shared cream wash (`_astrarium_paint_cream_wash`).

    **Music glyphs** come from the bundled Noto Music face, because a treble clef's spiral renders as mush when built from polygons. Its staff is one em tall (drawn at `size = 4 * gap`) and its baseline sits on the *bottom* staff line, so the clef is drawn with `anchor="ls"` onto that line; noteheads are ink-bbox-centred on their pitch instead.

    **Saturation.** Tier `0.5` (white ground; red is a small fraction of the page by design, and pushing it harder would blot the fine staff rules it sits on).

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - **An "i before a/e/o is a hiatus" syllable rule:** fixes `qui-et`, breaks `fri-end` / `be-li-eve` / `car-riage`, which are far more frequent. Measurement showed it cost more than it bought; the exception table carries `qui-et` instead.
    - **A "shift a one-letter head right" rule:** fixes `an-oth-er`, breaks `eve-ning` / `eve-ry-thing`. Also removed in favour of the exception table.
    - **Equal notes** (the first revision): a flat stream with no speech rhythm.
    - **Grow-only final bar.** Growing the last syllable without padding rests gave up whenever the remainder was not one of four values, leaving the last bar short on **51% of (row, meter) pairs** across the committed corpus — 83% at 12/4.
    - **A weighted random walk alone:** it wanders and then simply stops, which sounds aimless; the four rules above give it direction.
    - **Flag polygons on every eighth:** they looked like blobs at a 7 px staff gap.

    **Its own sleep frame** (`render_lieder_sleep`, the theme's `spec.sleep`): the manuscript puts itself to bed with the opening of Brahms's *Wiegenlied*, Op. 49 No. 4 (1868; words from *Des Knaben Wunderhorn*), headed "Wiegenlied" with the poet credit flush left and "Johannes Brahms, Op. 49 No. 4" flush right, as a song is headed. One system is engraved through the quote frame's own `_lieder_paint_system` (same clef, noteheads, beams, flags, augmentation dot, lyric face and final double bar) on a larger 12 px stave, because there is only one line of music to fit. It sits in 3/4, the song's meter rather than the hour, and below it the rest of the first verse is set in the editorial italic. "gut' Nacht" is sung in the theme's red under its slur, playing the part the matched phrase plays in the quote frame. **The melody is in C major** (the engraving has no key-signature machinery; Brahms wrote it in E♭) and was checked note by note against the well-known tune (from musical knowledge; no score was reachable when it was written, so a check against the Simrock first edition is still worth doing). `_LIEDER_SLEEP_MELODY` pins it as an upbeat of two quavers E E, then | G (dotted crotchet) E (quaver) E (crotchet) | G (minim), set to "Gu-ten A-bend, gut' Nacht," and a test pins pitches, durations and the full-bar arithmetic. **Simplifications:** only the first phrase is engraved, not the second ("mit Rosen bedacht"), because its syllable underlay could not be confirmed against a score and a wrong note in a famous tune is a visible bug. That line and the rest of the stanza are printed as verse text instead. Bar 2 closes with a crotchet rest where the next phrase's upbeat would fall, so the cut reads as a breath rather than a short bar. The pickup quavers are beamed across their syllables, following the theme's modern-vocal beaming convention rather than Brahms's 1868 separate flags. `time_str` is deleted at entry, so nothing on the frame tells the time. It is composed at 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled.

  - `izakaya` (black alley ground / white + yellow neon tube cores / blue + red blooms, Quicksand + Yuji Boku) — **a Kabukichō back alley after rain**, and the theme that introduces **glow** to the renderer. A custom-render frame, bypassing the literary layout, dispatching from `render` into `render_izakaya_frame`.

    **Why it exists.** Every other synthesised tone in this codebase mixes two inks at a *constant* density to fake a colour the panel lacks. A bloom is the opposite — one ink at a *falling* density — so the eye integrates a gradient out of a binary field. `paint_neon_mask` is the shared primitive: blur a glyph/shape mask (`ImageFilter.GaussianBlur`, the first use of `ImageFilter` in the renderer) and read the blurred field back as a per-pixel Bayer **density**.

    **Tuning the bloom.** Four facts, all learned against the panel and recorded in the primitive's docstring:
    - `radius` must stay **tight**, comparable to the stroke width and never to the text block — a wide blur of a dense line of text *is* a solid rectangle, so a wide radius paints a uniform slab behind the line and reads as a coloured box, not as glow.
    - `gamma` > 1 pulls the mid-tones down so the bloom decays over ~6-10 px instead of plateauing and then cutting off at a visible edge.
    - `cap` keeps the densest ring below solid so the halo always carries stipple texture.
    - Parameters tuned at 1:1 produce a bloom that **vanishes** at panel distance (box-average a finished frame to simulate 1-3 m viewing), so the near-ring density here is deliberately higher than it looks correct at pixel scale.

    **Composition.** Black ground under a faint blue night haze that thins toward the roofline (a gradient Bayer wash, the shape `chrono`'s sky uses; kept to a ~14% peak because at 31% the sky became the brightest thing on a page whose whole subject is light sources in the dark). Six vertical shop boards glow down the margins, three per side, each blooming its frame and its kanji from **one** mask so the housing and the tube wash into each other rather than double-exposing where two halos meet. Paper lanterns (提灯) hang above: silhouette bloom first with `core=None`, then the red body, then black cap / base / ribs as chords clipped to the ellipse, then the character.

    **Neon quote and street.** The quote is bent into neon through **two** masks — cool white-core/blue-bloom for the prose, hot yellow-core/red-bloom for the matched phrase — each blooming in a single pass, with the hot pass running last and allowed to overwrite the cool halo so the sung phrase reads as nearer. Wet asphalt below throws every sign and lantern back as a rippled vertical smear whose horizontal distortion grows with depth; the ripple modulates density, and `_IZAKAYA_STREET_Y` sits high enough that a reflection is taller than a sign column is wide — the condition for it to read as a smear rather than a bar.

    **Shared front half.** The wrap-into-two-masks step (prose in one `"L"` mask, matched phrase in the other) is `wrap_quote_into_masks`, shared with `abyssal` and `bakelite` — every *lit* frame needs the mask to exist before anything is painted, and the split has to happen there so each tier can take its own colour and falloff. Blooming per *chunk* instead double-exposes wherever two halos meet inside a line, which is the artefact this shape was built to avoid.

    **Ground set.** Every bloom passes `ground=_izakaya_ground()` (black + blue). Without it a later glow eats the tube cores, sign frames and lantern ribs an earlier pass already painted — the halos of adjacent sign columns overlap heavily.

    **The hour rides the main lantern as a kanji numeral** (一 … 十二) — a real lantern carries a shop's character, so a numeral there is the one place a digit belongs without breaking the fuzzy-clock premise; `time_str` is therefore used, as in `lieder` / `tarot` / `vitrail`, and the matched phrase still carries the readable time. Sign words are ordinary alley signage (居酒屋 / 焼鳥 / 酒 / 横丁 / 喫茶 / 営業中) and are deliberately **kanji-only**: katakana signage would want ー rotated in vertical setting, and a wrongly-oriented glyph is worse than not using one.

    **Distinctness.** Surfaces all six inks at once: black is the alley; blue the night haze above the roofline and the body text's bloom; white the neon tube cores (the glass is the brightest thing in a sign); yellow the matched phrase's tube core; red the matched phrase's bloom, the paper lanterns and two signs; green two more sign columns. Iconographically distinct from `outrun`, the other neon theme: outrun is a flat poster lit uniformly, izakaya is a place with light sources at different depths over a reflecting ground. Saturation tier `0.7` — a bloom is precisely what a low saturation flattens.

    **Tried and rejected.** Two bugs worth not reintroducing:
    - **Rippling reflections by *skipping* rows whose sine crested.** That removes the reflection across a column's full width at once and reads as stacked horizontal bricks, so the ripple now modulates density instead.
    - **A ~70 px deep street.** Shorter than a sign column is wide, so reflections read as bars; hence the high `_IZAKAYA_STREET_Y`.
  - `abyssal` (deep-sea gradient ground / white body / mint matched-phrase bloom, Lato) — **a quote read through deep water**, and the theme that finally claims the **seafoam** recipe. A custom-render frame, bypassing the literary layout, dispatching from `render` into `render_abyssal_frame`.

    **The recipe.** `spectra6_color_recipes.md` had listed seafoam (G+B+W @ 40/30/30) as "not in use / future ocean or spa theme" since the catalogue was written. The surface band is that mix, but *animated by depth* rather than held at a fixed ratio: a 3-way Bayer partition whose white and green weights fall linearly with y, so the water reads turquoise at the surface and pure blue by `_ABYSSAL_SURFACE_BOTTOM`. The peak allocation is **6 green / 5 blue / 5 white** of the 16 Bayer cells — the closest a 4x4 tile gets to 40/30/30, a 0.025 quantisation drift. `tests/test_render_quote_themes.py::TestAbyssalSeafoamMix` measures the bare water layer over whole Bayer tiles (a single row is not representative) and reads its target out of `_DIAGS_TRIPLE_SWATCHES`, so the recipe and its one consumer cannot drift apart.

    **Depth.** Below `_ABYSSAL_DEEP_TOP` blue flips to black on a rising ramp capped just short of solid — a fully black floor stops reading as water and starts reading as a border. It also fills a genuine palette gap: `anna_atkins` (flat Prussian-blue plate), `firmament` (navy sky) and `chrono` (twilight gradient) are all blue-ish, and none of them used blue as **depth**.

    **Caustics are a contour, not a peak.** `_abyssal_paint_caustics` paints a thin band around the *zero crossing* of an interference field (`abs(f) < eps`), which is a level set, which is a connected curve, which is a net. Two sines alone give a perfectly regular lattice that reads as tile grout; the third term at an incommensurate frequency is what warps it into the irregular closed cells real caustics form on a seabed.

    **Second consumer of the glow primitive**, and the one that proves `paint_neon_mask` generalises past neon: bioluminescence is precisely a falling-density bloom. The jellyfish are built as a *single* mask — bell arc, radial ribs and trailing tentacles together — so the whole animal shares one continuous halo; blooming the parts separately double-exposes where two halos meet, the lesson izakaya's sign frames taught. The bell is an **arc, not a filled dome**: a solid white disc reads as an opaque blob stuck on the water, where leaving it hollow lets the gradient show through and the animal reads as translucent. Every bloom passes `ground=_abyssal_water_ground()`, which is deliberately just `{blue, black}` — the caustic net, the marine snow and an already-painted jellyfish are all light *sources*, and letting a later halo repaint them would dim the very things the glow is meant to sell.

    **The hour is a depth.** `_ABYSSAL_METRES_PER_HOUR` is 500, so the sounding gauge runs 500 m at one o'clock to 6000 m at twelve — and 6000 m is the true floor of the abyssal zone the theme is named for, so the clock bottoms out exactly where the name does. That puts it with `lieder`'s time signature and `izakaya`'s lantern numeral rather than with the frames that `del`-assert `time_str`. The graduation numeral on the hour's own mark is suppressed, because the green marker label lands in the same place and the two overprint into mush — most visibly at twelve, where both read "6000".

    **Quote.** The quote takes the same cool/hot split izakaya uses: white cores throughout for legibility against the water, a blue bloom on the prose so it sits *in* the medium, a mint-green bloom on the matched phrase so the time reads as the one living light on the page. Saturation tier `0.7` — every lit element here is a falling-density stipple, which is exactly what a low saturation flattens.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - **A 4 green / 7 blue surface allocation** (G25/B44/W31): a substantially bluer surface that did not implement the recipe this theme exists to claim. The suite's only seafoam test checked the *diags swatch list's names*, not any theme's implementation — hence `TestAbyssalSeafoamMix`.
    - **Thresholding the caustic field high** (`sum of sines > k`): isolates the maxima into disconnected specks that read as confetti.
  - `pride` (rainbow cloth ground / black Jost body / violet matched phrase) — **the Progress Pride flag, flying**. A custom-render frame, bypassing the literary layout, dispatching from `render` into `render_pride_frame`.

    **Why this panel.** The flag is very nearly the one banner Spectra 6 can fly at native gamut, and it is the theme that surfaces the widest span of the documented palette: eleven bands, of which five are native inks (red, yellow, green, blue in the rainbow; white and black in the chevron) and four are already-documented two-ink recipes — orange is the R+Y 5/8:3/8 tangerine (`deco` / `atomic`), violet the R+B 1:1 (`alchemy` / `vitrail`), pink the R+W (`placard` / `chalkboard`), light blue the B+W 1:1 sky blue (`vitrail`), brown the R+G 1:1 sepia (`saloon` / `newsprint` / `tarot`). Every ink the hardware has appears at once, and no new recipe was invented to do it.

    **The chevron order is measured off the reference flag, not taken from memory** (two web searches gave two *different* wrong orders — one putting white between pink and brown, the other light blue innermost). The band boundaries sit at 0.148 / 0.223 / 0.303 / 0.383 / 0.465 of the flag's width, so reading inward from the hoist at mid-height the order is **white, pink, light blue, brown, black**, with the innermost white a filled arrow rather than a band (which is why its depth is not simply one fifth). The arms are straight — `x + |y - centre|` stays constant down an arm on the reference — which is why one distance term places a pixel in the whole nest of Vs.

    **The vertical term is scaled by the aspect ratio, and must be.** The depths are fractions of *width* while an arm's travel is vertical, so a literal 45 degrees puts the hoist corner in a different band on a canvas whose aspect differs from the reference's 1008x658. The reference corner sits at `0.5 × 658 / 1008 = 0.3264` of the width — inside **brown**; at the panel's 800×480 unscaled it lands at 0.300, inside **light blue**. `_pride_arm_slope` multiplies the vertical distance by `(width/height) × (658/1008)`, reproducing the reference proportion at every canvas size at the cost of the arms no longer being exactly 45 degrees in pixel space — which is correct, since a real flag reproduced at a different aspect has its arm angle change too. `tests/test_render_quote_themes.py::TestPrideChevronBands` fences the band order and each band's recipe against the canvas, pins the pink/light-blue pair both web sources got wrong, and asserts the corner lands in brown at five different aspect ratios.

    **Brown is R+G and is deliberately *not* muted with black.** The catalogue offers black as an optional mute for the sepia recipe; taking it up would make this band indistinguishable from the black band it sits directly against. The mix looks olive in an RGB preview and reads as a real brown on the panel, whose inks are muted — the calibrated red (~#62201E) and green (~#35563A) average to ~#4C3B2C against the flag's #613915. Do not "correct" it from a screenshot.

    **The shading is the new capability.** Constant-density two-ink mixing fakes a colour; `paint_neon_mask`'s falling density fakes a glow; this is the third form — **tonal shading of an arbitrary colour field by a low-density overlay of the two achromatic inks**, which is what lets the cloth read as lit rather than as a colour-bars test card. `_pride_wave` sums two incommensurate sines (a single frequency corrugates) and returns *both* the stripe displacement and its derivative from one pass, because the two are 90 degrees apart: the cloth's height displaces the stripes, but the surface *tilt* is what the light sees, so the bright band belongs on the slope of a fold and not its crest. The phases also drift with `y` so the folds lean rather than standing perfectly vertical. The stripe index is clamped, not wrapped, so where the wave carries the edge stripe off-canvas it simply continues — the frame reads as a crop of a larger flag.

    **One read, not two — and this is the load-bearing detail.** A Bayer tile has a fixed cell count, so any two reads of it are perfectly correlated and a phase shift merely selects a different fixed subset. `_pride_paint_flag` therefore resolves each pixel with a **single** read that partitions the tile three ways by rank: the lowest `round(density * 64)` cells take the lighting ink and the remainder splits between the stripe's two inks in the stripe's own ratio. Worst-case drift is 0.045, which is pure quantisation. `tests/test_render_quote_themes.py::TestPrideStripeInkRatios` fences this, and its canvas assertion samples the *extremes* of the wave rather than a wide average: the drift is equal and opposite on the two faces of a fold, so an average over many folds cancels it to nothing and passes against the bug.

    **`BAYER_8x8`, not `BAYER_4x4`.** The shared 8x8 tile (derived from the 4x4 by the standard recursive construction so the two cannot drift) exists for this frame and is the reason the folds read as cloth: 64 cells give the lighting 65 gradations instead of 17, where at 4x4 the shading quantises to five steps per fold whose boundaries read as hard contour bands. The finer tile also lets a two-ink mix land on its exact ratio (3/8 is 24/64 exactly, where 4x4 could only offer 6/16). Any future theme dithering a smooth *gradient* rather than a constant mix should reach for it.

    **The card is sized to its contents**, so a one-line quote gets a small card with plenty of flag around it rather than floating in an acre of white; `_pride_layout` measures everything before `_pride_card_rect` cuts the card and `_pride_paint_text` fills it. It is lifted off the cloth by a black drop-shadow ledge (the same ledge `kanagawa` uses to float its paper panel), without which it reads as a hole cut in the flag. The matched phrase is painted in the flag's own violet — on the card's white ground a 50/50 mix is right, unlike `outrun`'s magenta, which is red-biased only because it sits on a blue ground it would otherwise melt into.

    **Deliberately absent: any encoding of the hour in the flag.** The count and order of the bands carry meaning that is not a designer's to spend on a clock gimmick, so the flag is drawn as the flag and the time is carried by the matched phrase, as in `questline` / `outrun` / `marquee` / `chrono`; `time_str` is `del`-asserted at frame entry. The frame is otherwise unornamented on purpose — the flag *is* the furniture, and hanging corner devices off a flag would be the same category error.

    **Every card metric scales with the canvas.** `_pride_metrics` derives the pads, gap, corner radius, shadow offset, card minimum, text budget and font range from `min(1, width/800, height/480)`, capped at 1 so the native render is bit-for-bit unaffected. Without it a thumbnail gets a full-size card and full-size type on a quarter of the canvas (at 320×192 a 320×316 text block against a 244×176 card). The generic preview sweep renders every theme at 80×60 and 240×144 and asserts only `img.size` and palette-subset, which a frame whose text escapes its own card passes, so `TestPrideLayoutFitsEveryCanvas` measures the layout against the card directly. Credits are measured *before* the quote and their height reserved out of its budget (fitting the quote to the whole box and appending the byline afterwards pushes it off the bottom), and below `_PRIDE_CREDIT_MIN_SIZE` the byline is dropped outright rather than rendered at 5 px, where it reads as a rendering fault rather than an attribution. The 60 px-tall canvases still clip vertically and deliberately so — no legible layout of a literary quote exists in 60 px.

    **The card sits in the rainbow field, not on the canvas centre.** A centred card lands exactly on the chevron's point and swallows the gesture that distinguishes this flag, so `_pride_card_rect` centres it in the clear field right of `_pride_chevron_tip`. That costs text width — `_PRIDE_TEXT_MAX` is 322 px rather than the ~600 a full-bleed frame would allow — which the fit loop absorbs by wrapping to more lines and, for a dense quote, stepping the size down. On a canvas too narrow to hold the card clear of the chevron (the `/api/preview` thumbnail path) the clamp wins and the card overlaps: a legible quote matters more than an intact graphic at 80 px wide. Saturation tier `0.5` — the white card and the achromatic lighting dominate, and the bands are already at native ink saturation.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - *Picking the stripe ink by one Bayer read and then overwriting some pixels by a second read.* Wrong however the second read is phased: across all sixteen 4x4 shifts the lit face of the violet stripe came out at 0.27 or 0.73 red against a target of 0.50 — the hue sliding toward blue on one face of every fold and toward red on the other, a colour shift wearing the costume of shading (worst-case drift 0.23, against the single read's 0.045).
  - `pulp` (saturated yellow stock / black body / red accent, Alfa Slab One) — **a 1940s lurid paperback front** (issue #214). A custom-render frame; the cover furniture owns the canvas.

    **Distinct from `comic` by object, not palette.** `comic` is a *panel* — Ben-Day dots, racing stripes, a heavy gutter border, Bangers lettering. This is a *cover*: a red masthead strip (imprint + issue line), a huge uppercased cover title, an all-caps byline, the quote as the blurb on a knocked-out white band (black on saturated yellow is illegible at distance), a starburst price flash and an angled COMPLETE NOVEL corner banner. The saturated yellow ground is the only thing the two share, and even that differs — `comic`'s Ben-Day field is a red dot *triangle* in one corner, where `_pulp_paint_stock` lays a full-ground 1-in-16 black screen that reads as cheap paper without shifting the hue.

    **The misregistration is the point.** Pulp paperbacks were printed fast on bad presses and the giveaway is that the colour plates do not line up. `_pulp_misregistered_text` reproduces that literally — the red plate goes down at a `(3, 2)` offset and the black covers most of it, leaving a fringe on two edges. Order matters: red painted *after* black puts the fringe on top of the letterform and reads as a drop shadow rather than a printing fault. It is also why the theme needs a **fat** face — on a hairline serif the fringe eats the letterform instead of haloing it.

    **The time rides the issue line**, `VOL. XII · NO. 30` — hour as a Roman volume (reusing `_TAROT_ROMAN_NUMERALS`), minute as the issue number. Every real pulp carried volume and number on the cover, so this is period chrome rather than a digit bolted on, the same move `lieder` makes with its time signature. It is the most *direct* time surface in the rotation: `NO. 30` at half past is the minute in plain digits, where `lieder` hides its minute in a metronome mark. It earns the exception the way `lieder`'s hour does — a volume/number line is required furniture on this object and a reader parses it as a serial, not a clock — and the quote still carries the readable time.

    **The corner banner** is built on its own RGBA tile and rotated 45 degrees with **BICUBIC** and a hard-thresholded alpha: at 13 px a NEAREST resample shreds the glyph stems into noise, while an antialiased *silhouette* leaves off-palette fringe for the final snap to guess at. Saturation tier `0.7` (saturated non-white ground, same argument as `comic`).

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - *A red paper screen.* Red over yellow is the documented R+Y tangerine, so it would change the ground colour rather than dirty it, and collide with a recipe three other themes own.
  - `synoptic` (white ground / black body / red matched phrase, Space Mono) — **a meteorological surface analysis** (issue #215). A literary-layout theme with a heavy `draw_synoptic_border` painter and a `clear_rect` knockout, *not* a custom frame: a chart is a ground the quote sits on, not a composition that owns the canvas.

    **It completes the navigation family.** `firmament` is a celestial atlas, `astrarium` an instrument dashboard, `cartograph` a terrestrial chart. Where `cartograph` *describes* terrain — coastlines, form-lines, a compass rose — a synoptic chart is a field laid over geography: nested isobars, pressure centres and front symbols. No shared vocabulary.

    **The occluded front is the reason the theme is worth building.** Cold fronts are drawn blue with triangles, warm fronts red with semicircles, and an occluded front — a cold front that has caught up with and lifted a warm one — is drawn *purple*, carrying both symbols alternately. So the R+B 1:1 mix is **semantically** right here rather than merely convenient: the colour is purple on a real chart precisely *because* it is the two fronts merged, which is exactly what the two inks are doing. Nothing else in the rotation gets to use a synthesised colour for the reason the source material uses it. Measured on the canvas the front runs 47% red / 53% blue and box-averages to `(128, 0, 128)` at panel distance. It is painted in an off-palette sentinel and bbox-post-passed, because `ImageDraw` has no per-pixel fill and the mix has to survive both the line and the pip polygons.

    **The time rides the validity stamp**, `VALID 1630 LT` — every real chart carries the observation time it analyses, so this is a place a number genuinely belongs, the same category of solution as `izakaya`'s lantern numeral. It is labelled **LT**, not UTC as a real analysis would be: the clock renders naive local wall time (`run_clock.current_time_str` is `datetime.now()`), so a UTC label would be false on every appliance outside UTC — a device in UTC-4 showing 12:30 would claim an analysis valid four hours away. `TestSynopticValidityStamp` fences both rejected alternatives below. `time_str` reaches the painter only in `render`'s knockout pass (the spec sets `wants_time`); the plain pass the button-C source card makes passes no time, so the card gets a chart with no stamp, which is correct — a source card is not an analysis valid at a time.

    **Both `ornament` slots take the page ground on purpose.** The shared layout paints its oversized quote marks *outside* the body rect — so outside this theme's legend box — where a large glyph floating on the chart reads as debris rather than typography. A surface analysis has no decorative quotation marks, so here they are simply not drawn. The pressure centres sit in opposite corners so their tight inner rings land in the margin band the legend box leaves free, and the isobars are two low-order harmonics on the radius rather than ellipses or noise, which keeps the chart byte-deterministic. Everything is drawn with `ImageDraw` primitives, which PIL clips, so the `/api/preview` thumbnail path crops rather than raises. Saturation tier `0.5` (light ground).

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - *Converting the stamp's value to UTC.* This stamp *is* the time carrier, so a number disagreeing with the quote defeats it.
    - *Reading the host's real zone abbreviation.* That makes the frame machine-dependent, which is the hazard `CLOCK_DEPENDENT_THEMES` exists for.
  - `vhs` (black tape ground / white Antonio body / red+blue chroma ghosts) — **a worn VHS tape under a camcorder OSD** (issue #211). A custom-render frame (`render_vhs_frame`).

    **The fourth named ink technique.** `draw_text_dithered` synthesises a *colour* by mixing two inks at constant density; `paint_neon_mask` synthesises a *glow* by falling density; `pride`'s three-way rank partition synthesises *shading*. `draw_text_chroma_shift` synthesises a **defect** — the red and blue records of an analogue signal drifting out of registration, which is what makes a worn tape look worn. Three passes, and **the order is load-bearing**: the two chroma ghosts go down first at opposite horizontal offsets, then the core covers the middle, leaving a red fringe on one edge and a blue fringe on the other with clean text between. Painting the core first and the ghosts after puts colour *over* the letterform and reads as a coloured outline, not as a signal that has come apart. A `ground` frozenset restricts which inks the ghosts may overwrite — the same guard `paint_neon_mask` takes, so a later line's fringe cannot eat an earlier line's core.

    **Every ghost on a line is laid down before any core.** Per-chunk ghost-then-core only holds the clean-letterform-centre invariant *within* a chunk; a matched phrase splits a line into adjacent chunks, and the next chunk's left ghost lands on the previous chunk's tail. The `ground` guard cannot reject that, because `ground` necessarily lists every ink the frame paints — the white core included. At the shipped offsets (2 regular, 3 bold) the glyph side bearings absorb the reach and nothing is overwritten; it starts eating cores around offset 5, so this is a latent trap for anyone widening the bleed. `TestVhsChromaBleed::test_no_chunk_ghost_eats_a_neighbours_core` sweeps the offset to catch it, and drives the real `_vhs_paint_quote` rather than reimplementing the pass order (a test that replicates the ordering itself passes against the very bug it guards).

    **Distinct from `pulp`'s misregistration**: there two *ink plates* miss each other by a fixed offset in one direction, so the fringe is a printing fault; here the separation is symmetric about the glyph because a *signal* is splitting, not a sheet slipping. The matched phrase is emphasised by a **wider offset**, not a different colour — on a tape the thing that stands out is the thing that has degraded most.

    **Tape furniture:** scanlines deliberately fainter than `nightvision`'s (a terminal is *scanned*, tape *smears*); a noise field biased toward the foot (two samples, keep the lower — wear is worst at the head sweep, and uniform noise reads as a clean digital dither rather than a worn tape); sparse white dropout dashes where the oxide has shed; **tracking tears** that shift bands of rows sideways and are applied *after* the text, so a tear cuts through the quote rather than reading as a decorative stripe; and a band of **head-switching static** across the last few scanlines, the most recognisable playback artifact there is. The head-switch band is *repainted* as bright static before being torn — sliding the existing noise sideways just produces more noise.

    **Tears are bounded well below the body cap height.** A tear must look like the picture slipping, not like the quote being deleted, and `TestVhsChromaBleed::test_core_survives_the_tears` fences it — measured as the fraction of scanlines the tears *disturb*, not as ink lost, because a tear shifts a row rather than erasing it and pixel counts barely move however violent it is.

    **The time carrier is a camcorder burn-in**, and this is the one theme where surfacing plain digits is authentic rather than a violation of the fuzzy-clock premise: a camcorder OSD *is* a clock, and the burn-in is what a viewer of such a tape actually reads the time off. Wall time, not a tape-position timecode, because a tape counter is not a clock and the whole justification is that this OSD is one. The **date** stamp is deliberately not today's — deriving it from the clock would make the frame machine-dependent (the hazard `CLOCK_DEPENDENT_THEMES` exists for, and the trap `synoptic`'s validity stamp avoids), so it comes from `_row_digest` instead. That is also the more honest reading: the date on a burn-in is when the tape was *recorded*, not when it is being played, so a different quote is a different tape.

    **Composed at the canonical 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled** for a non-native request — the convention `metro` established, and required because the quote rect and the tear / noise geometry are fixed panel coordinates. Saturation tier `0.7`.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - *A tear band 11 px tall shifted 26 px* (the first draft) displaced a third of a line far enough sideways to make it unreadable.

    **Its own sleep frame** (`render_vhs_sleep`, the theme's `spec.sleep`): **the tape has run on into the station's sign-off.** Somebody taped the late movie off the air and the tape kept recording past it, so the night ends the way broadcast days ended: 75% colour bars across the top, the reverse strip under them pairing each bar with black, and a sign-off card ("THIS CONCLUDES OUR BROADCAST DAY" / **GOOD NIGHT** / "Programming resumes in the morning.") set in the quote's chroma-bled Antonio, with GOOD NIGHT taking the matched phrase's wider offset. It reads as a broadcast that has ended; a blue screen or snow, the obvious alternatives, read as a signal that has failed. The tape wear is the quote frame's own (`_vhs_paint_tape`, `_vhs_paint_dropouts`, `_vhs_apply_tears`), so it is visibly the same tape. The bars are painted over the tape ground, so the seeded noise (`_vhs_paint_noise`, split out of `_vhs_paint_tape` byte-identically) is laid again on top of them; the first cut left the bars cleaner than the card (caught in review). The scanlines are not repainted over the bars: faint on the black ground, they would rule dark lines across bright bars. The OSD turns from the camcorder's REC to the deck's PLAY ▶, keyed white on a black edge over the bright bars, and **no clock is burnt in**: this is the one theme whose quote frame prints HH:MM, so leaving the burn-in would make the sleep frame tell the time (`TestVhsSleepFrame` spies on every text call and allows no digit). The three bars the panel has no ink for are stippled: grey as W 3/4 + K 1/4, cyan as seafoam G+B+W at 40/30/30, magenta as R+B at 1/2 (reads violet on panel inks; the cyan bar is the weakest, a dusty blue-grey). **The card is placed around the tears.** The tears are seeded and land on fixed rows (305-327), and the card leaves that band empty: the first draft let them cross the small line, which read as strikethrough, and the second let them cross GOOD NIGHT, which shredded it past reading. `TestVhsSleepFrame::test_tears_spare_the_sign_off_card` measures the rows the tears actually move, so a change to the tear seed that lands one on the card fails. It is the brightest sleep frame in the set, with the bars lit across the top third. Composed at 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled; `time_str` is `del`'d.
  - `cardcatalog` (manila card / black Special Elite body / violet library ink) — **a library catalogue card** (issue #210). A custom-render frame (`render_cardcatalog_frame`), and of everything in the rotation the most on-brand: the one theme that is *about books being borrowed*.

    **A custom frame, against the issue's own note.** #210 proposed a literary-layout theme with a `clear_rect` knockout, the `kanagawa` / `cartograph` / `letter` pattern. That does not work: the shared layout centres its block over the page and at 800×480 the body rect runs 34–766 horizontally and 54–425 vertically, which leaves no right margin at all. The stamp column *is* the theme — composition and time carrier both — so the frame owns the canvas and the quote is laid into an annotation column beside the stamps.

    **The panel is exactly a card.** 800/480 reduces to 5/3, the ratio of 3×5 index stock, so the page is the card face at full bleed rather than a card drawn inside a drawer.

    **The time carrier is the freshest stamp.** Earlier impressions sit faded and askew with the ink density varying per stamp (a pad that dried out between borrowers); the current one is crisp, square to the card, spans both stamp columns, and reads `DUE` over the hour. A due *hour* rather than a due date is not a liberty — reserve and reading-room collections ran two-hour loans and stamped exactly that. The minute stays with the matched phrase, which is painted in the **same violet as the stamps** (R+B 1:1, the documented Tyrian recipe — library date stamps really were purple, and it keeps the theme off the white/black/red pile), so the readable time and the stamped one are visibly the same fact recorded twice. `TestCardcatalogDueStamp::test_no_minute_reaches_the_card` fences the hour-only rule by requiring every minute of an hour to render byte-identically.

    **The manila ground is the layered aged-paper recipe run light**: a Y+W cream Bayer wash under a sparse R+G sepia foxing scatter, the two-pass recipe `tarot` uses for vellum, at roughly a fifth the foxing density — a catalogue card is decades old and handled, not centuries old and archival. **The foxing lattice must stay coprime with the 4-pixel Bayer period** — two lattices sampling the same pixels alias unless their periods are chosen not to. `TestCardcatalogManila` measures the painter's output for both sepia inks, because a foxing pass that paints nothing still renders, snaps on-palette and passes the preview sweep.

    **The card invents no bibliographic data.** The tracing block at the foot lists a title added entry (which exists for every work), the holding collection, and the row's real Gutenberg ID as an accession number. Filling the foot with plausible *subject headings* would have meant fabricating catalogue data about a real book to make the card look better. The call number is derived from `_row_digest` — a call number is the one genuinely arbitrary thing on a card, so it only has to be *stable* per book — and is held inside Dewey 800–819, which is where every book in this corpus actually belongs.

    **Distinct from `dispatch`** despite sharing Special Elite (real cards were typed, and that is the bundle's typewriter). dispatch is a dossier: white ground, black frame, tractor-feed perforations, one crisp maroon archival stamp, "FILE COPY". This is manila-and-sepia with violet library ink, a call-number block, a rod punch-hole and a grid of overlapping impressions. Same typewriter, different object. Space Mono carries the call number and the stamp faces, which on real cards came off a metal type wheel rather than a typewriter, so the register shift is period-correct rather than arbitrary.

    **Composed at the canonical 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled** for a non-native request, the convention `metro` established. Every rectangle on the card is an absolute panel coordinate — a card is a physical object with fixed proportions, not a fluid layout — so a direct render at the curator grid's 320×192 would put the whole stamp column (x=582..770, the theme's time carrier) off the canvas and return a cropped top-left fragment, which the generic preview sweep (`img.size` and palette-subset only) passes. `TestFixedGeometryFramesDownscale` pins the downscale for every fixed-geometry frame, and pins NEAREST specifically — these frames are built from per-pixel stipples, and an interpolating filter would average adjacent inks into colours the panel cannot print. Saturation tier `0.5`.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - *Foxing sampled every other pixel at `(7x + 13y) % 24 == 0`.* That forces `x % 4 == y % 4` on every hit; both surviving diagonal cells of the tile sit below the cream threshold, so every one of the 8000 candidate positions had already been claimed by the wash and the foxing pass painted **exactly nothing**.
  - `bakelite` (warm sand moulding / brown scanline CRT / amber-phosphor readouts, Jost + Space Mono) — **a mid-century instrument console**, and the theme that teaches the renderer to synthesise a *glowing* colour. A custom-render frame: the housing is most of what makes a piece of period instrumentation read as an object rather than as a UI, and the shared literary layout leaves no margin for one — its body rect runs 34–766 across the 800 px panel.

    **Amber is the problem the theme exists to solve.** `paint_neon_mask` (the primitive `izakaya` introduced) blooms *one* ink at a falling density, which is all `izakaya` and `abyssal` need because their halos — blue, mint — are far brighter than the grounds they spill onto. There is no amber ink, and ranked by the panel's *measured* luminance the six are white 195, yellow 180, green 70, red 53, black 31: red, the obvious halo for orange, sits within a few points of the brown screen it is meant to glow against, and that screen's brown is *made of* red, so a red halo is indistinguishable from it. The fix composes the two techniques the codebase had kept apart — **falling density for the glow, a constant two-ink ratio for the hue** — and it lives in `paint_neon_mask` rather than in the theme, because a synthesised glow is a general want: the primitive grew `glow_minor` / `core_minor` and an optional finer tile, and `_bakelite_paint_phosphor` is a thin wrapper supplying this theme's inks. `izakaya` and `abyssal` render byte-identically across the change (their goldens pin it), since a single-ink glow is just the split with no minor. A lit character therefore steps gold at the stroke (Y+R 5/8:3/8), orange through the halo (R+Y 5/8:3/8) and brown into the tube. Two tiers: the prose has that gold stipple core, while the matched phrase takes a solid white core and a wider, denser bloom, an over-driven character on a one-phosphor tube.

    **The ratio has to ride the density's own Bayer read, as a split band** — the lit set at any point is the consecutive ranks `0..n-1`, and its lowest 3/8 take the yellow. That is `pride`'s rank partition applied to two inks, and exact by construction at any density except a very short run, where rounding up biases it a few points high (which is why the whole-halo average measures ~0.43 against a 0.375 target: a halo is mostly faint tail by area). Measured in annuli out from a blurred disc (a *curved* edge, so the sample crosses every phase of the tile — binning by distance from a straight edge aliases against it and reports nonsense), the split band holds 0.38 → 0.41. The same split carries the scanlines' 3:1 red:green and the core's 5/8 gold.

    **The screen's brown is the scanlines, not a wash.** Only every third row is painted, solid at the tube's centre, alternating red and green along its length; the two rows between stay pure black. The eye integrates that into a dark warm brown *with visible line structure*, which is what a shadow-mask tube looks like, and it carries the vignette for free — thinning the lit rows toward the rim darkens the corners the way a real tube falls off, with a mild lift toward the upper left standing in for the reflection in the glass. R+G is weighted 3:1 rather than the sepia recipe's 1:1 because the panel's green (#35563A) is a cool one and an even mix measures grey-green; and the vignette exponent is 3.0 (1.6 reaches only a third of the way in and leaves the whole screen a flat neutral).

    **The moulding is a jittered ordered dither** — the Bayer rank plus a positional hash at ~40% of a cell — over a roughly 9:4:3 white:yellow:red mix marbled by two slow sines; ordered-plus-jitter keeps the low local variance while dissolving lattice and banding. The mix was measured, not eyeballed: yellow-dominant snaps to a flat lemon, and straight Y+W cream lands on khaki, because it is the red that pulls the average onto a warm sand. Two bevels are cut into it — a raised outer lip and the sunken screen recess, opposite polarities under one upper-left light — each laid at a density that *falls* across the band so the faces read as a surface turning away from the light rather than as two drawn outlines.

    **The chrome is unlit, and that is the whole hierarchy**: labels are flat solid yellow with no bloom, so the silkscreened legend sits behind the emissive readout it names. Solid red would be the truer unlit amber but red-on-black is the lowest-contrast pair the six inks offer and 11 px letterspaced caps disappear into the scanlines; the R+Y tangerine that would split the difference shreds a letterform that small (the hairline-at-small-size failure `astrarium` and `vitrail` document). The rules are wider than a glyph stem, so they *can* carry the tangerine, and do. A cell whose corpus row has no value prints an em dash rather than vanishing — the reference instrument's own idiom for a sensor it cannot reach, and a bare row is common enough (the miner fills title and author only when the Gutenberg header carried them) to design for.

    **The hour rides a setting index**, `HOUR 2/12`, the way the reference shows a fan speed as a discrete step out of its range. Hour only: the minute stays with the matched phrase, so every minute of an hour renders byte-identically for a given row (pinned by `TestBakeliteHourIndex` — if a minute could reach the frame, the readout would be a clock). That puts it with `cardcatalog`'s due stamp and `abyssal`'s depth gauge rather than with the frames that `del`-assert `time_str`.

    **Shared plumbing.** The mask-building front half — wrap the quote, split prose from matched phrase into two `"L"` masks — is `wrap_quote_into_masks`, shared with `izakaya` and `abyssal`; all three present the quote as light rather than ink, and a bloom needs the mask before anything is painted. Composed at the canonical 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled for a non-native request, the convention `metro` established. Saturation tier `0.7` — every lit element is a falling-density stipple, which is exactly what a low saturation flattens.

    **Tried and rejected.**
    - *Single-ink amber glows.* Red at a cap of 0.7 vanished into the scanline field; yellow alone (the only ink bright enough to carry a falloff unaided) reads olive, because the panel's yellow is a markedly green one.
    - *A second Bayer read for the hue split.* Any second read of a fixed tile is perfectly correlated with the first and merely selects a subset — the halo's near-black tail came out pure yellow and its bright ring pure red, so the glow changed hue as it faded rather than dimming.
    - *Residue-class one-read rules.* `rank % 8 < 3` is exactly 3/8 over a whole tile but drifts 0.39 → 0.51 across the falloff, and `(rank * 3) % 8 < 3` swings 0.36 → 0.44 → 0.33. Both fail because a residue class of the 8×8 tile is not spatially neutral — the recursive construction encodes each cell's quadrant in its low bits.
    - *Moulding by ordered dither alone* is smooth but lays a visible lattice that reads as a screen door (the artefact `kanagawa` leaves the grid to avoid) *and* quantises the marbling into hard-edged blobs (`pride`'s contour banding). *Hash alone* has no period to lattice but reads as sandpaper across a large pale field.
    - *Rolling the ordered thresholds with sines* — a trap: two periodic patterns beat, and the slab came out in a regular diagonal plaid.
  - `metro` (white map stock / black Jost body / red matched phrase) — **a metropolitan transit diagram**: a custom-render frame whose four full-palette route lines own the canvas.

    **The network.** Routes use cased strokes and strict horizontal / vertical / 45-degree geometry; white station nodes and ringed transfer markers stay legible over every native ink. The quote is a central service-notice card, and the matched phrase is the red express line through its text block.

    **The time is wayfinding furniture.** The hour becomes a circular line number and the minute a stop code. `_metro_paint_network`, `_metro_paint_quote_card`, and `_metro_paint_chrome` are kept separate for structural mutation tests.

    **Type and geometry.** Jost provides the legible geometric signage face, its open counters surviving route-map legend sizes, and its true Bold gives the matched phrase an interchange label's weight; Space Mono carries agency chrome. Non-native preview sizes render the canonical 800×480 map and downsample with nearest-neighbour so fixed route geometry never inverts or clips. Saturation tier `0.5` (white ground).

    **Its own sleep frame** (`render_metro_sleep`, the theme's `spec.sleep`): the same map on its night timetable. The three day routes are drawn **hollow**, two thin rails of their own ink with white between and hollow ink-ringed stations (`_metro_paint_suspended_route`). That is the diagram convention for a line that exists but is not running, so the network still reads as a map in its own colours rather than as a stroke that failed to fill. Greying them by stipple was the obvious alternative, and the hollow line is what a real map prints. The blue route, which the day key already names NIGHT, is the one still running: round blue dots at an even 17 px pitch, measured continuously across corners so a joint never bunches them (`_metro_dotted_points`), on a white casing laid over every day line. A service notice ("Night Service", a run of night-line dots, "Day lines resume in the morning.") replaces the quote card. The notice uses the same card shell, set lower and shorter (`_METRO_NOTICE_CARD`) so the suspended network and three of the four interchanges stay in view above it. The masthead reads NIGHT SERVICE, a blue **N** night-line roundel and an ALL NIGHT plate take the hour and stop code, and the key splits into NIGHT · RUNNING and DAY LINES · RESTING. It prints no resume time, because the renderer does not know `--quiet-end`. `time_str` is `del`'d, and the frame contains no digits at all (`TestMetroSleepFrame` spies on every `text` call). It is composed at 800×480 and NEAREST-downsampled like the quote frame. The grid, interchanges, masthead and card shell are shared with the quote frame through `_metro_paint_grid`, `_metro_paint_interchanges`, `_metro_paint_masthead` and `_metro_paint_card_shell`, factored out byte-identically.
  - `nocturne` (black night / blue brushwork / synthesised-gold light, Cormorant Garamond Medium) — **Whistler's *Nocturne in Blue and Gold***, the Thames at Battersea, and the theme that introduces flow-field stroke rendering.

    **Painted, not toned.** `paint_flow_strokes` advects short streamline strokes through a caller-supplied direction field (origins jittered by integer position hash, strokes integrated segment-by-segment so they bend with the field), so the water reads as marks with direction and rhythm rather than as tone. A custom-render frame (`render_nocturne_frame`).

    **Sky, water, shore.** Sky strokes are sparse, thin, near-horizontal, thinning with altitude; water strokes are denser and wider with a shimmer term that calms with depth, roughly one in seventeen flipped to green in the mid-water (the verdigris glaze). The shore is painted *after* the strokes as a solid silhouette band (plus the Battersea shot tower and a chimney), so land reads as the absence of light and crops stroke overhang. The horizon haze is a `BAYER_8x8` density ramp with the rank jittered by the position hash (an unjittered ordered ramp lays a visible dot lattice and a hard-edged onset — the bakelite moulding lesson).

    **All the gold blooms through one recipe.** Shore lights, their swaying reflections (a third `paint_flow_strokes` pass with a near-vertical field, confined by its `ink_at` to columns under each light), the Falling Rocket's spark shower (deterministic parabolic fan, one mask, one bloom), the matched phrase, and the butterfly monogram bottom-right all bloom through the split-band synthesised gold `paint_neon_mask` grew for `bakelite` (R-major tangerine halo, Y-major gold core, 8×8 tile), each tier one-mask-one-bloom with `ground=_nocturne_ground()` so later light never eats earlier light.

    **Hierarchy.** Prose gets solid white cores in a faint cold blue halo (radius 3, cap 0.35 — the abyssal posture); the matched phrase is the brightest gold after the rocket, the painting's own hierarchy. The quote block sits deliberately left-of-centre so the spark shower owns the upper right, as Whistler composed it. Credits are small, solid, black-stroked white in the darkest water — unlit by design, bloom-vs-no-bloom being the hierarchy.

    **`time_str` is `del`-asserted** — a nocturne is one suspended moment with no in-fiction instrument to hang an hour on; the matched phrase carries the time alone, pinned by a byte-identical-across-all-times test. Saturation tier `0.7` (black ground, falling-density blooms).
  - `plaque` (dark verdigris-bronze ground / relief-lit burnished-brass lettering / white speculars + black core shadows, Cinzel Decorative Bold) — **a patinated bronze memorial plaque**, and the theme that introduces **relief lighting** (`paint_relief_mask`): text as a raised, lit surface, where every earlier treatment is flat ink, a stipple or a glow.

    **The primitive.** `paint_relief_mask` reads the blurred glyph mask as a height field and lights its gradient from the house upper-left (the `vitrail`-came convention). Its `shade_face` switch: wide shapes (the cast border bead, the four corner bolts) bevel their own faces, but thin letterforms keep a solid metal face and take their lighting entirely on the exterior, because shading a 2-3 px stroke's own face decays the glyph into a grey ghost. A custom-render frame (`render_plaque_frame`).

    **The ground is dark because nothing lighter can carry text.** It is **dark verdigris** (G+B+K ~33/17/50), which is also the truer object: a weathered bronze tablet's recessed field goes dark with corrosion while the raised letters stay burnished — exactly the figure/ground split this theme needs. Green takes about two thirds of the non-black cells rather than half, because an even split reads **navy** — the panel's blue (#233F8E) is far more chromatic than its desaturated green (#35563A) and wins the perceived hue at equal share. On an RGB preview the ground reads lime — trust the panel's muted inks, the `pride`-brown warning.

    **Corrosion moves hue, not luminance.** Two sine fields swing green against blue with the tile read hash-jittered so corrosion pools without latticing (the bakelite moulding lesson), at *fixed black*, so the swing moves hue while the field's luminance — the axis legibility is actually made of — stays pinned; face contrast holds 6.1-6.4:1 across the whole sweep. **Swing a 3-ink ground's two cuts together, or the variation lands on the ink you did not mean.**

    **Burnished brass inscription.** The inscription is Y+W: 3/4:1/4 for the prose at 6.28:1, and 2/5:3/5 for the matched phrase at 6.60:1 — the passage every thumb has kept rubbed to bright bare metal — under a deeper relief cut. Their luminances are close, so the phrase's step is carried by hue (warm to cool), the deeper cut and the heavier Cinzel weight rather than by brightness alone. The documented Y+R gold stays on the bead and bolts, decoration with no contrast to earn. The whole Cinzel chain steps one weight heavier than usual (Bold body / Black phrase) because a relief face needs stroke mass to live in.

    **The `contact` crease.** Every glyph carries the occlusion line where a raised letter meets the plate — new to `paint_relief_mask` and off by default so `daguerreotype` is untouched. Lambert shading alone leaves the two arcs lying *along* the light (a glyph's upper-right and lower-left) with a near-zero gradient and no shading, so the form touches the ground with nothing between them; the crease closes the contour. Against the dark ground it is a modest gain rather than the load-bearing one (it darkens the ground's brighter green and blue pixels immediately around each letter, moving 1.16% of the canvas) — kept because it is cheap, physically right, and still adds edge contrast. Its width is a floor on the halo and must be read off a **stroke**, not a broad edge: one pixel outside a 3 px stem the radius-2 halo is only ~93, so a threshold of 96 creases nothing — silently, since the render still succeeds and stays on-palette.

    **Dedication and attribution are cast raised too**, in the same brass under the same light, subordinate by size rather than by being a different kind of mark. One light, one metal, two sizes.
